This may be why paradoxically, the production of pop culture has been so vital to American power. In most societies, entertainment is not that important as a source of meaning as people have tradition and religion and custom. But in America, entertainment plays a bigger role than something people do in their free time. Because if society is a game, music, films and television provide a root of shared experiences to bridge such a diverse society, and up till recently these have all been able to leverage America's vast internal market to gain popularity at home, and then export worldwide. People in America need not believe in the same God, but they can watch the same shows, sing the same songs and read the same comics. This may be why the age of social media has been so damaging to American social life.

The former which was to reveal false consciousness and foment worker consciousness and often were of social and historical realism. On the other hand, American media, literature, and film in this era were not as thematically deep or artistic. This is why you rarely study American art, film, and literature in this era because it was just entertainment or pop culture, although there were American art, literature, film produced that are considered art in this era, but these are not widely known by people even Americans. In Soviet era, art, film, media were part of the social cohesion of society and not pop culture like in US. You can argue that American media served its ideological and social purposes as well. But you are ignorantly viewing this era from an American arrogant lens that was probably kindled by this discussion of how America won the media wars.

> ✝️ The Bible (Old & New Testament)  
>   
> Yes, it has multiple versions and translations.  
> 	•	Written over ~1,500 years by many authors  
> 	•	Originally in Hebrew, Aramaic, and Greek  
> 	•	Thousands of manuscripts exist with small differences (wording, verses added/removed, order changes)  
> 	•	Examples:  
> 	•	Some verses appear in later manuscripts but not earlier ones (like the long ending of Mark)  
> 	•	Many translations (KJV, NIV, ESV, etc.)  
>   
> Scholars agree:  
> 👉 The message is largely preserved, but the text has gone through editing, copying, and translation changes.  
>   
> ⸻  
>   
> ✡️ The Torah (Hebrew Bible)  
>   
> Often seen as more stable than the Christian Bible, but:  
> 	•	Exists in different textual traditions:  
> 	•	Masoretic Text (Jewish)  
> 	•	Septuagint (Greek)  
> 	•	Samaritan Torah  
> 	•	These versions have differences in wording and structure  
>   
> So technically:  
> 👉 There are variants, but Jewish scribes were extremely strict about copying accuracy.  
>   
> ⸻  
>   
> ☪️ The Qur’an  
>   
> Islam teaches that:  
> 	•	The Qur’an has been perfectly preserved since revelation to Muhammad  
> 	•	Standardized under Caliph Uthman (~650 AD)  
> 	•	Same Arabic text used worldwide today  
>   
> Historically:  
> 	•	Early manuscripts (like the Birmingham manuscript) match modern Qur’an closely  
> 	•	Some early variant readings (qira’at) existed, but within controlled boundaries of pronunciation and dialect  
>   
> So:  
> 👉 Among the three, the Qur’an shows the highest textual uniformity.

### **@lhta9550** | 👍 4 likes
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=ijnkCt1QK6k&lc=UgwRANjH8J3Z4K1mKLJ4AaABAg)

This professor‘s presentation is particularly effective because he consistently applies simple presentation techniques that unfortunately very few people follow. One is to tell the audience what you’re gonna tell them, tell them, and then tell them what you told them. In short he makes it easier to remember by repeating three times. Second,  within each concept or topic he’s breaking the argument down to no more than three points. More than that would tax our concentration. Third, he explains at a high school level (even though some terminology is more advanced and specialized), which makes it even easier to reach adults. Fourth (see I broke the rules already), these concepts are compelling, humanistic and spoken to eager ears in the most teachable moment. His genius: know the subject, care about the learner’s needs, and show enthusiasm. One of those high IQ people who can explain things simply. A true study in effective presentations.

>
> ​@numbersix8919 The US export economy and bond market are kind of a self-fulfilling cycle. The US consumer market is the largest in the world so it can absorb a lot of exports from other countries. Because it's importing more than it exports in aggregate, that means other places end up with US dollars in exchange for their exported goods. They can buy some stuff with it (like oil) but usually they need another way to spend it, and US treasury bonds were the answer. Because the US was so stable and strong you could use your extra USD to buy US government bonds, it was a safe place to park the money, you made a small return on it, and countries need to buy and hold bonds and debt anyway just to enable budgeting and finance, pensions, etc.   
>   
> This in turn has meant that since there were so many long term consistent buyers for US bonds the cost of borrowing remained extremely low, which is how the US has been able to borrow and amass so much debt. If there wasn't as much 'built-in' demand for US bonds the rates would have had to be higher (because less demand means you have to offer higher returns to buyers so they will buy), which would mean the financing costs would be more, which would have limited US borrowing due to higher interest costs. It was never sustainable, but whatever debt crisis we have yet to reach would have long passed without that 'built-in' demand for US bonds.

So it wasn't really the people that wanted to leave, it was the elite that wanted to leave to gain more control and more leverage over the now isolated population of the UK. Leaving the EU "freed" the UK from EU regulations, allowing a closer alignment with the USA and the collective push for -- to use Yanis Varoufakis' term 'technofeudalist' ideals. The population, don't forget, are not the elites and secret societies running things like their own personal playground. 

That said, I can confirm that most English speakers find it hard to learn other languages, I think for two main reasons. First, the culture of ridicule for trying to speak another language is sky high in English speaking countries in my experience. Second, if and when you do try to speak French or Spanish or German etc. to native speakers of those languages, they notice you're an English speaker and just speak English to you instead. I have many times had to insist that we speak German or French so that I can practice!

27:17 
As a black person who's lived in America for my entire life I must point out that America's appreciation for merit and fairness is fickle in reality. Those ideals in practice exist mostly a marketing ploy to attract human resources via immigration. Given how the Manifest Destiny expansion took place I think it make sense that enough of the founders/American elite had imperial ambitions from the beginning. 

When I think about America as it functions, most of the appreciation for merit and fairness within American culture comes from working class folks who've bought into that narrative, which has been promoted since the nation's founding. Because of this there is a legitimate culture of merit and fairness but it's not as strong as it could because it's constantly at odds with cultures of racism and classism, which have also existed since the nation's founding.

The civil war was more about establishing a centralized federal government that could override state power. But the but the anti-slavery movement is treated as the main focal point/reason for the Civil War.

TL;DR - the elites use narratives about cultures of merit and fairness to encourage immigrants to play the American game.

### **@waightforrest1737** | 👍 3 likes
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=ijnkCt1QK6k&lc=UgxMLV4yTlm797nF2Ox4AaABAg)

---
  
"So… I want to talk to you today about something we all live inside of, but almost nobody can see.

Reality.

Not like… rocks and trees reality.
I mean the reality we agree is real.

Because here’s the uncomfortable truth:

Modern reality works like a Ponzi scheme.

And I know that sounds dramatic—
but stay with me.
Because I’m not saying money is a Ponzi scheme.

I’m saying the entire cultural operating system is.


---

Think about how a Ponzi scheme works:

It doesn’t run on value.
It runs on belief.

It only survives if:

new people keep buying in

the story stays convincing

and nobody asks too many questions


Now take that…
and apply it to everything.


---

Let’s start with the American dollar.

We used to pretend it was backed by gold.
Then we were like:

“Nope. We’re done with that.”

And we replaced it with something far more powerful:

Narrative.

We made the dollar backed by:

oil

global trade

military protection

institutions

and most importantly…


the story that it is inevitable.

The story that it is stable.
The story that it is the default.

And it works—
because the world is basically forced to participate.

Which is a very polite way of saying:

> “This is the game. Play it. Or starve.”




---

Now here’s where it gets really weird.

Someone once said:

America’s number one export is entertainment.

And that sounds like a fun fact…

…but it’s not a fun fact.

It’s a warning.

Because entertainment isn’t just movies.

Entertainment exports:

lifestyle

identity

status

aspiration

what “freedom” looks like

what “success” looks like

what “love” looks like

what “cool” looks like


America doesn’t just export products.

America exports the template for what reality is supposed to feel like.


---

And that brings us to Baudrillard.

Hyperreality.

That’s the moment where: the image becomes more real than the real thing.

The copy becomes the original.

The map becomes the territory.

The trailer becomes better than the movie.

The Instagram life becomes better than the life.


---

And once you live in hyperreality…

you don’t need truth anymore.

You need vibes.

You need aesthetics.

You need branding.

You need the story.


---

Now here’s the part that broke my brain:

It doesn’t just work on other countries.

It works on us.

The people who live here.

We are not just selling the dream.

We are trapped inside it.


---

And I’ll prove it with the simplest example:

If you tell everyone you have the best burger in the world…

…and they eat it…

…and it’s average…

It doesn’t feel average.

It feels like betrayal.

Because the hype raised their expectations so high
that the normal thing becomes disappointing.

That’s hyperreality.

And that’s Ponzi logic.

Because now you have to keep raising the hype.

You have to keep escalating the story.

You have to keep making it bigger.

Because the moment people stop believing…

the value collapses.


---

And that is the system we live in.

A society where:

Reality isn’t measured by what’s true.

Reality is measured by what people can be convinced to accept.

Where the illusion has to get louder every year
to cover the fact that it’s getting emptier.


---

So no, I’m not saying “everything is fake.”

I’m saying:

We’ve built a world where belief is monetized,
attention is extracted,
and the story has to grow forever…
or the whole thing breaks.

That’s not just hyperreal.

That’s a Ponzi scheme of reality."

18:30 As an American this is a very interesting framing of the relationship between Britain and the 13 colonies and what led to our war of independence. Our cultural narrative of it is almost exclusively linked to being under or unrepresented in parliament and having no mechanism to meaningfully advocate against unfair taxation and abuses of authority by the crown (the eventual 'Bill of Rights' specifically outlines the most significant systemic abuses). Obviously the trends and issues Prof. Jiang cites were also factors but they are rarely ever mentioned as being relevant drivers to our push for indepdence.

### **@doomedbook1020**
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=ijnkCt1QK6k&lc=UgydEv2gLMinj_vbahZ4AaABAg)

America is going to change the game again. Allow trade in each country's currency but use an agnostic bridge currency to facilitate real time swaps between currencies, that America technically owns due to the distributing country being America based. Each country, instead of owning dollars will own a portion of the escrow of this currency, which has a deflationary mechanism that is linked to transaction volume. The more the world trades, the more of the bridge currency is burned and the more stores of value (stocks, bonds, commodities, real estate, etc) linked to the bridge currency to be traded between one another in real time, increases that burn rate, therefore making all the countries in the world richer simultaneously as the value of the bridge increases. This encourages further co-operation with America without the hard economic sword that pushes countries against America, as America retains soft economic power while allowing other countries the ability to economically self determine (theoretically). Since 2008 (and before) America has been secretly working with the financial institutions globally to implement this system behind the scenes. We are witnessing greater de-dollarisation through America playing devils advocate against the world in order to implement this new financial system. The main catalysts are the Yen carry trade unwinding, War with Iran, the Ukraine/Russia war and the Israel/Gaza war, all leading to greater distrust of the dollar. Once America attacks Iran, the Strait of Hormuz will be closed, causing Japanese oil prices to skyrocket, forcing Japan to raise interest rates even further, and accelerating the unwind of the carry trade where dollars are sold in return for JPY. Because the current system can't handle such a huge moving of capital, the new bridge currency will be brought in to facilitate the historical trade between USD to JPY, and also set the precedent to stabilise exchange rates between all the worlds currencies, while still maintaining US financial hegemony.

The Tiananmen Square massacre refers to the bloody crackdown on pro-democracy protesters by the Chinese government on June 3–4, 1989, following weeks of demonstrations that began after the death of reformist leader Hu Yaobang in April 1989. 

Events and Crackdown
Martial law was declared on May 20, 1989, and by the early morning hours of June 4, troops and tanks moved into central Beijing, engaging in violent clashes with demonstrators along Chang'an Avenue and in the Muxidi suburbs, rather than solely within the square itself.  The government labeled the movement a "counter-revolutionary rebellion," and the crackdown resulted in the suppression of the protests, the exile or imprisonment of leaders, and the purge of officials like Zhao Ziyang. 

Casualty Estimates and Aftermath
The death toll remains disputed:

Official CCP tally: 241 dead. 
Independent estimates: Ranging from 300 to 2,700 dead. 
Western diplomats and media: Estimated hundreds to thousands killed, with up to 10,000 arrested.

if you really think about it, the gold is also a ponzi scheme. Just like a dollar bill can be used to take notes, gold can be used to make circuit boards and for some reason humans are attracted to it for its own sake.

The gold standard was always notional, and this should be obvious. Real wealth after all comes from extracting resources from earth to satisfy human needs and greed. Innovation. Labour. Entrepreneurship.

Removing the gold standard essentially helps put a higher multiplier on the ponzi scheme - increases velocity of the game, if you will. The trouble is that in a globalised exchange economy, political power determines value of exchange. So we have countries whose raw material and agricultural produce does not command enough exchange value.

USD is currently the most universal measure, scale or yardstick of denominating value. And so wealth compounds the fastest wherever the exchange value is more favourable.

### **@alfredhitchcock45**
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=ijnkCt1QK6k&lc=Ugw0ox2fjxc_8gBKhXl4AaABAg)

The Exorbitant Privilege of USD as Global Reserve Currency

Ever wonder why America feels so "liquid" — like money flows like water (parang tubig)? Generous 30-day returns, easy loan approvals, obscene valuations for apps that seem useless, sky-high NBA salaries, massive CEO pay + stock options, endless Amazon shopping, consumer culture, even quantitative easing... These aren't just "American innovation." They're rooted in the exorbitant privilege of the USD being the world's dominant reserve currency.

The world (central banks, governments, investors) hoards dollars and US Treasuries for trade, safety, and reserves. This lets the US borrow super cheaply, run huge deficits without immediate crisis, export inflation, and keep interest rates low. Result? Cheaper mortgages, credit cards, imports → abundant goods, high asset prices, wealth effects for the middle class, flexible businesses that afford generous policies, and a high-liquidity environment where risk-taking (startups, consumption) gets rewarded big time. It's not just elites — everyday Americans benefit from easier homeownership, cheaper stuff, stronger 401(k)s, and overall "abundance" that feels unique.

No one else has this. Europe has the euro (2nd biggest reserve), but no unified fiscal authority, fragmented markets, and fiscal rules prevent the same deficit-spending flexibility. China? Huge economy, but yuan not fully convertible, capital controls, and trust issues block full replication. Even big players can't copy it — the US setup (single sovereign, deepest markets, historical inertia, willingness to run deficits) is one-of-a-kind.

But it's eroding slowly. IMF data (Q3 2025) shows USD share in global reserves at ~57% (down from 70%+ in 2000). Dedollarization via BRICS (local currency trade, yuan oil deals, CBDCs) is real, though gradual — no overnight collapse. If it accelerates and USD loses reserve dominance:

Borrowing costs rise → tighter credit, less easy loans, slower growth.

QE gets riskier → less "printing" without inflation spikes.

Consumerism tempers: higher prices for imports, businesses cut perks like generous returns/trials.

Valuations compress: no more obscene multiples for speculative apps; investors get risk-averse.

Now imagine that PLUS a return to the gold standard (dollar tied to fixed gold amount, money supply capped by gold reserves). Money becomes scarce and rigid — no flexible Fed printing during crises. Historical gold eras had deflation, higher unemployment, deeper recessions, more volatility.

In that world:

Obscene app valuations? Gone — cheap capital vanishes, bubbles deflate, focus shifts to fundamentals.

NBA stars at $50M+ contracts? Salaries shrink in real terms — league revenues drop with slower growth, less disposable income.

CEO mega-pay + stock options? Curtailed — lower stock multiples, tighter margins, boards cut excesses.

The "liquid" America we know — abundant credit, materialism, excess — dries up. More scarcity, austerity, discipline. No full collapse (US still has innovation, markets), but the unique advantages fade hard.

It's a privilege that's permeated society... but privileges can end. What do you think — is dedollarization a real threat, or overhyped? Or would a gold standard fix things?

#ExorbitantPrivilege #USdollar #ReserveCurrency #Dedollarization #BRICS #GoldStandard #GlobalEconomy #Economics #Finance #AmericaFirst #PhilippinesPerspective

### **@downtourth** | 👍 2 likes
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=ijnkCt1QK6k&lc=UgzvgtJo4Rnwuwk-guV4AaABAg)

An American Experience Living in Iran, late 1970's

My stepdad worked for Bell Helicopter, he was chief of facilities for BH at the Khatami Air Base in the desert. We lived in the city of Esfahan, not on the base like most of the Americans, my mom insisted on it. We really got to know the Iranian people very well. Though I missed my family in the states, I have fond memories.

For one, the Iranian people are the most hospitable people I have ever met, it's almost like they have a competition on who can be the most hospitable, lol. And very respectful to each other, that is important there. They are so misunderstood now because of their authoritarian government. The people welcomed the western international community at the time to help westernize and modernize the country before the uprising. At the time I did not know the back story which I won't get into now..

Fresh flat bread made every morning on our corner, baked in a hot river rock oven, sometimes a rock would still be stuck to the bread, baker didn't knock off so I always double checked, I bit into more than one over the 3 years lol. 

Every morning fresh river water from the Zayandeh Rud River and dam system would flow through the city's jubes, gulley's along side all the streets, which kept the city green and clean. And evrything is always kept clean. Iranians are always sweeping and washing down around their homes, inside is always immaculate. If it's a dirt floor, that gets swept daily also. 

Unfortunately, the river has experience heavy drought in recent years. 

The bridge of 33 arches is amazing, spans over the river and a popular gathering spot at night to enjoy the rush of the water and the night sky. Vendors cook corn set right on hot coals, then dipped in butter, it was my fav. 

The Persepolis ruins of King Darius monumental architecture and stone carvings, which Rome or Greece had nothing over. They put on a sound and light show at night recreating the invasion of Alexander the Great in 330 BC, invading and destroying the city as revenge of the Persians destroying Athens in 480 BC. I never seen anything like it before or since.

Hiking in the desert mountains, and stumbling across a beautiful oasis, we thought is was a mirage at first, just like in a movie, lol. 

It was unbelievable, ice cold water coming from the ground, running about 50 yards through trees, bushes, and sound of running water in the middle of a hot desert nowhere land. 

And yes we drank some, still the best water I ever tasted, we filled our canteens. Then, a goat herder shows up out of the blue to water his herd, he was so nice, bowing, smiling ear to ear, and shaking our hands. We offered him a burger and he gracefully accepted and said he would eat later because it was Ramadan, a religious ceremonial time of year they fast during the day for a month. We finally made it to the top of the mountain, my friends dad, who was an army Colonel, pushed us to near exhaustion, including the dog, and he was a badass army trained German Shepherd, lol. 

And then there are the Bazaars, my Mom's dream come true. 

A covered market place with mud, camel dung and hay dome buildings and tents that stretch for miles in all directions. Omg, she would drag me around for 6, 8 hours and longer sometimes, because we would get lost every time, the Iranians would be so helpful with my nonchalant Mom asking for directions to get back to our car, seeing me tired and frantic lol. My feet so sore by the end. But the Iranian food so good, that kept me going. The hand made objects from brass, copper, wood, clothes, Persian rugs, food courts, lots of spices and their aromas, you name it, I still have hand made brass copper urns and dishes from those shopping trips. 

And when the uprising started in 1979 before we could be evacuated, there were mobs in the streets, gunfire, tank return fire, it was crazy, but our Iranian neighbors protected us until we could leave safely. I'll never forget that. 

So many great experiences in Iran, and so much I have left out as to not write a book, but I felt I should share a little snippet to give a different aspect of what it is like there and how the people really are, besides what you hear on the news. Most of the country then and now want to be free from an authoritarian regime, like all people should be.  Iranians are such a happy, funny, vibrant, caring, cultural, strong people, whatever happens, I wish the very best for them, and someday I hope to visit Iran again, and go back to my childhood town of 3 years, Esfahan. 

Shake hands with old friends, get lost at the bazaar, hike to the the oasis, visit Persepolis, walk the 33 arch bridge at night, think of Mom, and gaze up at the stars..

### **@Synthstrucplexfyimmunosystemci**
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=ijnkCt1QK6k&lc=Ugxb6hEdSoXD3VgGwRV4AaABAg)

Sigh… this really made me pause. It almost feels like America wasn’t created for people as much as it was created to test whether a money system could work—like a giant experiment, or a dream meant to run long enough to record history. It makes me wonder if there’s ever such a thing as “good” history, or if it’s always built on trade-offs and quiet damage.

The way the world was divided—resources from Russia and the Global South, manufacturing from China, knowledge from Europe, finance from America—sounds efficient on paper, but deeply costly in reality. Cars everywhere, warmer winters, no snow anymore… the environmental bill keeps coming due.

Even recycling feels conflicted now. We tell ourselves it helps, but it still requires energy, transport, extraction, and waste. Sometimes it feels like the world is already too far gone to truly care, or at least too locked into habits to change. And even if we did change now—would it actually be enough? Sigh… I honestly don’t know.

Europe’s knowledge, China’s factories, Russia sending out raw materials as if the land is already spent—it all feels less like cooperation and more like managed decline. Everyone playing their role in the game, even when the game itself is breaking the environment and reshaping daily life.

This lecture didn’t give comfort, but it did give clarity. If this really is a “game,” then it feels like we’re living through a reset—one where the rules are being questioned, rewritten, or forced to change whether we like it or not.

I fight with these same doubts in my own life too. Thanks for the video—it gave me a lot to sit with. ^_^

LoL. China did act as a massive physical shock absorber for global commodities, but the US Federal Reserve actually saved the structural plumbing of the global financial system. And it's not like China did this because there was some sorts of round-table hold took place that said, hey, you need to save the world. Let's take a step back to see what 2008 looks like. 
When Western consumer demand collapsed during 2008 GRC, tens of millions of Chinese factory workers faced immediate unemployment. As the main exporter of the world, China couldn't solely rely on domestic consumers of goods themselves. They mainly rely on selling stuffs for Westerners, thousands of factories and millions of workers did. So basically if all Western country couldn't buy their stuffs -> their factory couldn't sell -> millions of workers faced unemployment. You can see similar thing almost happened again during the tariff period. Lots of factories collapse because they couldn't sell (but due to high tariff -> less competitive pricing rather than no one could buy it like 2008). To prevent a complete social collapse, Beijing unleashed a ~4 trillion RMB stimulus package. This professor frames this as a benevolent rescue mission, well, it's really not the case. It is pure pragmatism, and there is no shame in saying that. China fired a massive financial bazooka to keep their own people employed and their regime secure. But typical propaganda hidden inside sophisticated logic.

### **@charlesmiller7433**

Alright here’s the long form bare knuckle hot take. The real collapse of society is not the economy or the climate or the politics those are the symptoms because the collapse is emotional illiteracy causing an implosion under years of training people to equate survival with obedience or domination.
 
The way it works is you grow up in a family system where boundaries and emotional processing are almost never modeled in prohuman ways because you are taught early that your emotions are too much or inconvenient and so you learn to shut the f*** up or risk losing support 

and so then you get tossed into schools where teachers function as low level managers teaching you to perform compliance not curiosity and the lesson deepens that your value is how well you suppress your own brain signals to match external emotionally illiterate standards 

and then you graduate into jobs where the power structure crystallizes with the cost of saying no is homelessness or starvation or humiliation and your entire well-being hinges on the submissiveness of your nervous system to power and you are rewarded for silencing yourself and punished with financial ruin for asserting your consent and so people might carry that same emotionally illiterate script into relationships because how the f*** could they not because society provides almost no emotional literacy training on how to form meaningful emotional connection with others that is not based on implied threats of capitalistic emotional abandonment instead of on shared soul-level resonance then everyone wonders why dating is collapsing and why some marriages are hollow and why birth rates nosedive. 

it is because people no longer associate connection with nourishment they associate it with domination and dehumanization. Society thinks relationships are just natural bro don't think too hard like if you just shove two people in a room they will know how to navigate their complex lived experiences to connect meaningfully on a soul-level but that is magical thinking because emotions are complex and if you have spent your whole life training your nervous system to survive through capitalistic obedience and casual abandonment narratives then people are likely going to require education in how to notice and articulate emotional signals or how to talk about boundaries or how to build shared meaning or how to sit with emotions without gaslighting yourself or your partner and none of that is taught anywhere. 

So people outsource it to capitalistic power hierarchies or jobs or religions or corporations or therapy or apps or influencer scripts all of which recycle the obedience and abandonment logic and then people wonder why it feels like something is missing because the missing thing is the emotional literacy itself.

When you are born into this vacuum and parents are already overwhelmed and emotionally illiterate then cries for resonance are dismissed or minimized because the parent has no bandwidth to process that so the cycle repeats and each generation becomes a little more alienated until eventually birth rates collapse because who the f*** wants to pass on the suffering on an industrial scale. People would rather get pets or scroll themselves into oblivion because at least a cat doesn’t demand emotional resources you don’t have and at least a timeline full of memes doesn’t punish you for failing to communicate what you were never taught in the first place. 

This is not a fertility crisis it is a collapse of meaning. it is the spiritual death of connection. it is the realization that society has trained you to be a worker drone with no emotionally empowering toolkit. 

And if people keep thinking the solution is subsidies or family tax credits then the collapse persists because the real problem is emotional literacy not money. Until people are taught to process emotions without coercion every attempt to fix society will be a bandage on a severed limb.

### **@spacesnow21**
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=ijnkCt1QK6k&lc=UgwzNfoRryKe3tRmWOt4AaABAg)

I think you have a blind spot when it comes to how money and wealth work. The limited supply of gold is precisely why gold became the world money, instead of platinum or another, more readily available commodity. Creating infinite money doesn't mean infinite wealth. Money and wealth are completely different things. Wealth is the amount of products and services you have access to. If you give everyone a lot of money without increasing the amount of products and services, you create inflation. In other words, everyone becomes millionaires but remains poor, because the amount of products and services is still the same. The reason England dominated the world is because it was the first to industrialize. Industrialization increased productivity about a thousandfold compared to the rest of the world. They were able to produce many products very efficiently using the steam engine and factories, making England the richest and most powerful nation of its time. But slowly, neighboring countries like Germany and France learned/copied the technologies like the steam engine and factory production and also became rich and powerful. Later, Japan and the US joined the industrialization movement and also became powerful and wealthy. China has industrialized rapidly in recent decades. Given its population size, there's a good chance that China will become the next superpower. India could overtake China, but industrialization isn't that simple; you need stable and clear rules and regulations for taxes and other business activities. And, most importantly, an average IQ of 100 or more for your population. India has many intelligent people due to its huge population, but their average IQ is very low. Therefore, it's unlikely that India will be fully industrialized within a few decades.

Fiat money like the US dollar hasn't made any country richer, because creating dollars with computer keystrokes doesn't generate new products and services. However, it does allow the government to levy taxes on people who use their currency without realizing it. This has made the state much more powerful than when gold was the money. The US dollar currently has the most users of any currency in the world. That's why the US has been able to spend so much money on its military in recent decades. They tax the entire world, because most of the world still accepts dollars for their products and services. To prevent the US from taxing the entire world, we need a new global reserve currency. We could go back to gold, but the reason gold lost its reserve status remains unfixed. Gold is physical, and our modern economy is increasingly digital. You can't send gold over the internet. You can send a token representing gold, but then you have to trust a third party to be honest about the ratio between the token and the underlying gold reserve. In other words, the transaction is not final. For this reason, I personally believe Bitcoin will become the new global reserve currency, as it has a limited supply, can be divisible infinitely, and can be transferred over the internet. Only fully decentralized crypto currency. Most people will say, “But what about those thousands of other cryptocurrencies?” Other than Bitcoin, all other cryptocurrency are not sufficiently decentralized enough. Money is a winner takes all game. The chances of other crypto have more decentralization than bitcoin is very unlikely. why would anyone choose the second best if you can choose the best. The chance that other cryptocurrencies will be more decentralized than Bitcoin is very small. Why would anyone choose the second-best option when they could choose the best and thus strengthen Bitcoin's lead?
